• Baum → fungiert als → Integrator 
Quelle 
Grabner, Michael (2018): Dendrochronologie – Der Kalender im Baum. Vorlesungsfolien, Institut für 
Holztechnologie und Nachwachsende Rohstoffe, BOKU Wien. 
 
Kapitel 3: Auswertung von Jahrringen und Merkmale  
Structured Summary 
Das Kapitel verbindet methodische Grundlagen der Jahrringanalyse mit deren Anwendung zur Umwelt- 
und Klimarekonstruktion. Ausgangspunkt ist das Crossdating, eine zentrale Technik der Dendrochro-
nologie, mit der Jahrringfolgen verschiedener Proben miteinander abgeglichen werden. Durch den Ver-
gleich charakteristischer Muster der Ringbreiten lassen sich Jahrringe jahrgenau datieren und Fehler wie 
fehlende oder falsche Ringe erkennen. Die Zusammenführung vieler synchronisierter Proben führt zur 
Bildung von Masterchronologien, die als zeitliche Referenz für unbekannte Hölzer dienen. Auf dieser 
sicheren zeitlichen Basis lassen sich Jahrringmerkmale interpretieren. Eigenschaften wie Jahrringbreite, 
Holzstruktur oder Dichte reagieren sensibel auf Wachstumsbedingungen und spiegeln sowohl günstige 
als auch stressreiche Phasen wider. Zusätzlich treten spezielle anatomische Merkmale auf, die Hinweise 
auf Störungen liefern. Damit stellen Jahrringe eine Schnittstelle zwischen biologischem Wachstum und 
äußeren Einflüssen dar . Diese Zusammenhänge werden in der Dendroklimatologie systematisch ge-
nutzt, um Klimaeinflüsse auf das Baumwachstum zu analysieren. Jahrringdaten, ergänzt durch Holz-
dichte oder stabile Isotope, erlauben Rückschlüsse auf Temperatur- und Niederschlagsverhältnisse ver-
gangener Jahre. Voraussetzung dafür ist eine sorgfältige Standardisierung, um altersbedingte Trends zu 
entfernen und das gemeinsame Klimasignal zu verstärken. Schließlich zeigen Verwundungen, dass auch 
mechanische und biotische Einflüsse Spuren im  Jahrringbild hinterlassen. Insgesamt verknüpfen die 
behandelten Konzepte Datierung, Merkmalsanalyse und Umweltinterpretation zu einem integrierten 
Ansatz, der Bäume als langfristige Archive von Klima- und Umweltgeschichte nutzt. 
Knowledge Cards (4 pieces) 
Crossdating und Skeleton Plots 
Crossdating bezeichnet die systematische Methode zum Vergleichen und Synchronisieren von Jahrring-
verläufen verschiedener Holzproben. Ziel ist eine jahrgenaue Datierung der Jahrringe sowie das Erken-
nen fehlender oder falscher Jahrringe. Entscheidend ist dabei nicht die absolute Jahrringbreite, sondern 
der charakteristische Verlauf der Breitenabfolge. Besonders schmale Jahrringe, sogenannte 

--- PAGE 82 ---
Weiserringe, eignen sich gut zur Synchronisation. Eine wichtige visuelle Hilfsmethode sind Skeleton 
Plots, bei denen auffällige schmale Jahrringe grafisch hervorgehoben werden. Dadurch lassen sich Mus-
ter zwischen Proben vergleichen und standardisieren. Dur ch die Kombination vieler korrekt datierter 
Einzelproben entsteht eine Masterchronologie, der Kalenderjahre zugeordnet sind. Diese Chronologien 
können sehr lang sein und dienen als Referenz zur Datierung unbekannter Proben.

Jahrringmerkmale 
Jahrringmerkmale beschreiben die Wachstumseigenschaften eines Baumes. Die Jahrringbreite reagiert 
auf Umweltbedingungen. Zentrale Kenngrößen sind Jahrringbreite, Frühholz- und Spätholzbreite sowie 
die Holzdichte. Spezielle Merkmale, wie ein falscher Jahrri ng, sind durch Crossdating erkennbar. Ein 

--- PAGE 83 ---
fehlender Jahrring verursacht Zählfehler. Dichteschwankungen spiegeln Klimastress wider. Reaktions-
holz entsteht durch mechanische Belastung. Harzkanäle weisen auf Stress oder Verletzung hin. Kambi-
umstörungen entstehen durch Frost, Insekten oder Verletzungen.

Dendroklimatologie 
Die Dendroklimatologie untersucht die Beziehungen zwischen Klima und Baumwachstum. So reagiert 
die Jahrringbreite auf das Klima und spiegelt Veränderungen von Niederschlag und Temperatur wider. 
Neben der Jahrringbreite liefert auch die Holzdichte wichtige Informationen, da sie Temperaturbedin-
gungen widerspiegelt, insbesondere während der Spätholzbildung. Ergänzend dazu liefern stabile Iso-
tope zusätzliche Klimainformationen, etwa zu Wasserverfügbarkeit oder Temperaturverhältnissen. Eine 

--- PAGE 84 ---
zentrale methodische Voraussetzung ist das Crossdating, das eine jahresgenaue Datierung der Jahrringe 
ermöglicht. Um langfristige biologische Trends zu reduzieren, entfernt die Standardisierung den Alter-
strend aus den Zeitreihen. Die daraus gebildete Chronologie verstärkt schließlich das gemeinsame Kli-
masignal.

Verwundungen 
Verwundungen betreffen das Kambium, die Krone und die Wurzeln von Bäumen. Verwundungen ent-
stehen durch Frost, Hagel, Blitzschlag oder Insekten. Auch mechanische Belastungen verursachen 

--- PAGE 85 ---
Verletzungen, die häufig zu einer verstärkten Harzbildung führen. Eine Schrägstellung von Bäumen 
bewirkt die Ausbildung von Reaktionsholz. Verwundungen der Krone äußern sich durch Entnadelung, 
wodurch die Assimilationsleistung des Baumes reduziert wird. Wu rzelverwundungen entstehen unter 
anderem durch Erosion und Bodenverdichtung und beeinflussen die Standfestigkeit des Baumes. Insge-
samt hinterlassen Verwundungen charakteristische Spuren in den Jahrringen.

Chapter 7: The Stand-Based Silvicultural Management System: An In-
troduction 
Structured summary 
This chapter presents Stand-based silvicultural Management Systems (SSMS). These systems 
classify forest ecosystems into categories. The main factors for categorization are: tree density, 
species composition, diameter distribution, and the natural regeneration status of trees. The goal  
of this method lies in the optimal fulfillment of different forest functions, namely the economic 
and the ecologic functions of forest ecosystems which have to be balanced against each other 
in order to provide optimal results in both areas. The chapter furthermore lays out the six fun-
damental principles on which Stand -based silvicultural Management Systems are built which 
shall be elaborated upon in the subsequent knowledge cards.  
 
Knowledge Card 1 
Stand-based Silvicultural Management System (SSMS) 
The Stand-based Silvicultural Management System (SSMS) is a framework for managing nat-
ural forests by first classifying stands using specific factors such as tree density and then adapt-
ing operations to those conditions. It balances conservation and production through low-inten-
sity, stand -structured interventions that maintain continuous cover and support ongoing re-
newal. In practice, single-tree selection is used as the main harvest approach, with cutting rates 
aligned to growth to preserve structure and seed sources. A consistent workflow links planning 
to field action: classify stand types, inventory and review conditions, mark trees to cut or retain, 
harvest carefully, and monitor outcomes. This makes decisions easier, management scalable, 
and adapted to each stand’s structure, ensuring biodiversity, resilience, and steady timber yield 
over time. 

--- PAGE 87 ---

• Stand classification 
• Tree density

• Single-tree selection

ture, 2026, p. 101-116 
 
Knowledge Card 2 
History of the SSMS 
Historically, SSMS emerged from efforts to manage natural forests using tailored methods that 
respect stand structure and encourage regeneration. Early trials demonstrated that classifying 
stands and applying selective, low -impact treatments could sustain both ecological functions 
and timber production. Over time, practical adoption across larger areas refined the approach, 
integrating lessons from challenges such as inadequate regeneration under understory compe-
tition. Continuous cover ideas influenced the system’s preference for single-tree selection and 
avoidance of extensive clearcutting. As field experience accumulated, operational rules and 
workflows were standardized, improving consistency and outcomes. Today, SSMS is 

--- PAGE 88 ---
recognized as a long-term, adaptive model that uses simple, stand-focused rules to balance bi-
odiversity, regeneration, and yield in complex natural forests.

• Experimental trials 
• Practical adoption

Knowledge Card 3 
Fundamental Principles of the SSMS 
SSMS are grounded in six guiding principles tht provide the operational rules. Managers guide 
stands toward and maintain a preclimax state, avoiding intensive operations that broadly disturb 
soils, microclimate, and habitat. Actions are adapted to each sta nd’s structure and site condi-
tions, emphasizing tall, multistoried canopies that support biodiversity, stable microclimates, 
and continuous regeneration. Superior trees are retained to anchor genetic quality, seed supply, 

--- PAGE 89 ---
and future structure, while mixed conifer –broadleaf composition is fostered to improve resili-
ence and ecosystem functions. Together, these principles ensure low -impact, structurally in-
formed treatments that sustain renewal, productivity, and ecological int egrity across cutting 
cycles, linking day-to-day marking and harvesting to long-term stability and yield.

• Low-intensity operations

• future quality 
• species mix 
• Biodiversity 
 
Relationship Triplets

• Low-intensity operations ➜ reduce ➜ Ecosystem disruption 
• System adaption ➜ changes ➜ operations 
• Multistoried canopies ➜ enhance ➜ forest functions 
• Superior trees ➜ improve ➜ future quality 
• species mix ➜ builds ➜ Biodiversity

Suzuki, Satoshi N, Dai Fukui, Toshiaki Owari, and Nobuaki Tanaka: Integrative Forest Man-

Chapter 8: Implementing the Stand -Based Silvicultural Management 
System 
Structured Summary 
Chapter 8 lays out how to implement Stand -Based Silvicultural Management Systems in for-
estry practice by example of prior implemetation at the University of Tokyo Hokkaido Forest. 
The basis of this procedure consists of the categorization of forests into s ix specific types: Se-
lection Cutting Forest, Young Growth Forest, Sparse Forest, Secondary Forest, Artificially Re-
generated Forest, Restricted Forest. Firstly, it is important to classify and map out the forest. 
Subsequently, operational procedures and management as well as harvesting systems are to be 
selected in accordance with the fundamental principles of  Stand -Based Silvicultural Manage-
ment System and the forest category previosly seöected. The entire process must be accompa-
nied by supervision in order to ensure and assist the regeneration of the ecosystem.  
 
Knowledge Card 1 
Forest type classification and mapping 
Implementing SSMS begins with classifying stands into forest types using observable structure: 
tree density, species mix, diameter distribution, and natural regeneration status. Classification 
creates units that share similar treatments, making planning efficient at enterprise scale. Bound-
aries are surveyed with GNSS and laser compass and mapped in GIS, while airborne LiDAR, 
orthophotos, and recent UAV photos improve accuracy by revealing canopy height, terrain, and 
vegetation patterns. After classification, plots estimate growing stock, density, species compo-
sition, size distributions, and juvenile counts to assess regeneration. Field data is  recorded dig-
itally to allow analysis and prescription design. These maps and inventories form the opera-
tional backbone, ensuring that decisions are grounded in current conditions and that subsequent 
marking and harvesting can be targeted to support regeneration and stand health. 
 
Source 
Suzuki, Satoshi N, Dai Fukui, Toshiaki Owari, and Nobuaki Tanaka: Integrative Forest Man-
agement and Silviculture: Harmonizing Conservation and Production. Singapore: Springer Na-
ture, 2026, p. 117-136 
 
 

--- PAGE 91 ---

Knowledge Card 2 
Operational procedures and selection cutting 
SSMS use a standardized workflow to move from plan to practice: classify forest types, conduct 
plot inventories, hold in -field review meetings, mark trees, harvest, and supervise operations. 
Natural forests are managed predominantly with single-tree selection, harvesting at short, con-
sistent intervals with a small, steady cutting rate. The canopy gaps created by selective removals 
are filled by natural regeneration, maintaining continuous cover and avoiding extensive site 
disturbance. Management units are o rganized into cutting cycles and cutting rates are set to 
align removals with growth to preserve structure and seed sources. The in-field review validates 
that prescriptions match actual stand conditions before marking proceeds, and supervision dur-
ing harvest ensures layout, machine trails, and protection measures minimize damage. 
 

--- PAGE 92 ---

Knowledge Card 3 
Tree marking, assisted regeneration, and supervision 
Tree marking targets removals that improve forest health and future structure: trees affected by 
disease or insects, declining individuals with small crowns, and old, overmature stems unlikely 
to last to the next cycle. Selections are made within mapped harvest areas so interventions align 
with structural goals and regeneration needs. Where natural regeneration is insufficient, assisted 
measures combine site preparation such as surface treatment and removal of dense understory 

--- PAGE 93 ---
with low-density conifer planting. However, homogenizing canopy layers can increase wind 
vulnerability, so operations prioritize minimal removals and gradual transitions toward selec-
tion forests. Harvesting supervision checks execution quality, placement of machine trails, and 
damage control; compensation or remediation can follow if harm occurs.

• Harvesting supervision ➜ limits ➜ harvesting damage

Chapter 10: Management Practices for Natural Regeneration and Plan-
tations 
Structured summary 
Chapter 10 points out that even with the Stand-based Silvicultural Management System, natural 
regernation not always succeeds. Wile natural disturbances and understory competition is a 
general issue in most forest ecosystems, in the presented case in Japan  natural regeneration is 
mostly threatened by dwarf bamboos. In order to ensure natural regeneration in spite of these 
adversities, forest management measures specifically adapted to the local ecosystems are nec-
cessary. The chapter also explores forest management techniques such as polyculture planting 
and mixing tree species to increase resistance to disturbances such as wind throw. Furthermore, 
experiments with breeding different tree species have been conducted for the same raeson.  
 
Knowledge Card 1 
Assisted Natural Regeneration 
ANR is a method to accelerate natural regeneration. The method removes barriers and strength-
ens drivers of establishment. Barriers include dense understory, thick litter, and browsing. Driv-
ers include seed supply, safe microsites, and partial shade. Seed sources define the potential for 
recruitment. Safe microsites are mineral soil patches with moderated microclimate. Site prepa-
ration reduces competing cover and exposes soil. Spot or patch scarification creates seedbeds. 
Selective tending releases target se edlings from competition. Temporary protection limits 
browsing damage. Thresholds define success for density and composition. Monitoring checks 
thresholds and triggers follow -up actions. Supplemental planting is a backstop when seed or 
establishment fails. The approach preserves canopy structure and soil integrity. The approach 
focuses effort on desired cohorts. The approach builds feedbacks between retained seed trees, 
moderated microclimates, and rising seedling diversity. 
 
Entity List 
• Understory competition 
• Natural regeneration 
• Site preparation 
• Soil disturbance 
• Seed sources 

--- PAGE 95 ---
• Selective weeding 
• Supplemental planting  
• Regeneration gaps

• Site preparation ➜ reduces ➜ Understory competition 
• Soil disturbance ➜ reduces ➜ Natural regeneration

• Selective weeding ➜ reduces ➜ Understory competition 
• Supplemental planting ➜ fills ➜ Regeneration gaps

Suzuki, Satoshi N, Dai Fukui, Toshiaki Owari, and Nobuaki Tanaka: Integrative Forest Man-

Knowledge Card 2 
Patch scarification 
Patch treatments are small -area interventions. The goal is to create a mosaic of light and soil 
conditions. Small canopy gaps maintain continuous cover. Gap geometry controls light, mois-
ture, and root competition. Patch scarification breaks dense ground layers. Scarification exposes 
mineral soil and warms the surface. Microsite heterogeneity supports mixed -species cohorts. 
Planting near stumps uses low competition and partial shade. Few seedlings per stump can be 
sufficient. Spatial rules set patch size, sp acing, and rotation. Disturbance limits constrain cu-
mulative area and frequency. Machine trails avoid new cohorts and fragile soils. Mapping aligns 
patches with selection cutting units. Trade -offs are explicit and managed. Larger, brighter 
patches favor pi oneers and faster growth. Smaller, shaded patches favor tolerant species and 
lower risk. Monitoring adjusts patch design between entries.

Knowledge Card 3 
Polycultures 
Resilient plantations use diversity by design. Planting density is low to moderate. Species mix-
tures replace single-species blocks. Species complementarity is a design criterion. Complemen-
tarity includes rooting depth, phenology, and crown form. Spatial pa tterning creates structure. 
Rows, clusters, or ribbons form layered canopies. Cohort staggering spreads age classes. Natu-
ral ingress fills niches between planted trees. Early weed control is focused and time -bound. 

--- PAGE 97 ---
Selective thinning guides crown layering and stem quality. Protection targets high -value trees 
at risk. Performance indicators measure both yield and stability. Indicators include survival, 
growth, damage rates, and species balance. Management nudges stands toward polyculture over 
time. The result is predictable yield with higher resistance and recovery.

• Competition

• Performance indicators

• Species complementarity ➜ enhances ➜ Resource use 
• Planting pattern ➜ creates ➜ Layered canopies 
• Selective thinning ➜ improves ➜ Tree quality 
• Performance indicators ➜ evaluate ➜ Yield and stability over time

agement and Silviculture: Harmonizing Conservation and Production. Singapore: Springer Na-
ture, 2026, p. 151-175 
 
Chapter 11: Seedling Production 
Structured Summary 
Chapter 8 is about the production of seedlings under a Stand -based Silvicultural Management 
System. As explained in chapter 10, supplemental planting is employed in order to facilitate 

--- PAGE 98 ---
natural regeneration, especailly in small canopy openings. In this process, the seedlings are 
deployed to the forest after a growth period of 6-7 years. There are different options: on the one 
hand, there are containerized seedlings, which are currently be ing experimented with at the 
university of Toky, Japan. On the other hand, there are bare -rooted seedlings, which make up 
the major share of seedlings in use. The seedling production is however threatened by biotic 
and abiotic threats such as fungi, frost, heat or drought. 
 
Knowledge Card 1 
Seed sources 
 
Seed production for native conifers uses local orchards and designated seed stands. Orchards 
are clones from superior trees. The aim is open‑pollinated seed with low contamination. Seed 
stands provide cones where orchards are absent or unsuitable. Cones are collected in mast years, 
often late summer. Seeds are dried, cleaned, de‑winged, and stored cold. Cold storage maintains 
viability for many years. Genetic stewardship prioritizes local origin and within‑lot diversity. 
Mother trees are selected for health , form, and adaptation. Nursery plans link seed sources to 
sowing and raising schedules. Timing aligns phenology, storage windows, and field demand. 
The result is reliable, diverse, and adapted seed flow into beds and containers. 
 
Entity List 
• Seed stands 
• Tree selection 
• Mast timing 
• Processing and storage 
• Diversity management 
 
Relationship Triplets 
• Seed orchards ➜ supply ➜ local seed 
• Seed stands ➜ provide ➜ cones 
• tree selection ➜ determines ➜ clones 
• Mast timing ➜ targets ➜ late summer 
• Processing and storage ➜ preserve ➜ viability 
• Diversity management ➜ maintains ➜ representation 

--- PAGE 99 ---
 
Source 
Suzuki, Satoshi N, Dai Fukui, Toshiaki Owari, and Nobuaki Tanaka: Integrative Forest Man-
agement and Silviculture: Harmonizing Conservation and Production. Singapore: Springer Na-
ture, 2026, p. 176-191 
 
Knowledge Card 2: Bare-root produtction 
Bare-root production follows a staged schedule. Sowing occurs in autumn to use natural strati-
fication and spread labor. The seedbed phase runs two years for early growth and screening. 
Density is thinned to target seedlings per area. Transplanting moves se edlings to beds at fixed 
spacing. Root pruning in years three and four controls height and builds fine roots. Outplanting 
in autumn transfers stock at six to seven years. Operations set explicit densities, timings, and 
quality thresholds. The workflow balances height control with root development. The workflow 
links nursery calendars to field windows and site readiness. The outcome is robust stock with 
balanced tops and roots.

Knowledge Card 3 
Containerized seedlings 
Containerized production uses air‑pruning cells. Air‑pruning prevents root looping and forms 
stable plugs. Seedlings shift to containers after two years in beds. Greenhouse culture standard-
izes irrigation, fertilizer, and photoperiod. Cold storage enables flexible planting and safer lo-
gistics. Year‑round planting is possible where transport and site access allow. Survival often 
improves due to root protection and clean media. Growth may lag if initial height is lower, so 
raising periods adjust. Risk managem ent targets damping‑off, mold, frost, heat, and drought. 
Controls include seed treatments, spacing, drainage, shade, and irrigation. The objective is con-
sistent quality, high survival, and reliable field performance.

Chapter 12: Effects of Forest Management on Ecosystem Processes 
Structured Summary 
Any measure of forest management influences the ecosystem. This is specifically the case with 
larger practices, such as the use of plantations or clearcutting. The amount of dead wood is 
severely reduced by employing such management practices. This in turn  influences plants and 
animals alike, many which are dependent on an abundant stock of dead wood in the forest. 
Furthermore, the forest floor is affected, for example by the descturcion of saplings in growth. 
Because all processes in an ecosystem are interconnected, a decline of the amount of dead wood 
can initiate chain reactions that ultimately lead to adverse effects on the forest.  
Knowledge Card 1 
Dead wood 
Dead wood is a structural resource. It functions as habitat and regeneration substrate. Many 
organisms depend on its volume and continuity. Conifer regeneration, especially spruce, often 
recruits on logs and stumps. Selection harvesting can reduce dead woo d below natural levels. 
Retention restores substrates and habitat structure. Retention includes leaving downed logs, 
standing snags, and cull sections. Regeneration planning links species needs to substrate supply. 
Monitoring tracks volume, decay classes, and spatial distribution. Thresholds guide minimum 
volumes per hectare. Operations adjust when volume or decay diversity declines. The aim is 
dual: sustain biodiversity and secure regeneration niches. The result is a stand that couples pro-
duction with habitat function.

• Dead wood 
• Biodiversity 
• Natural regeneration

Knowledge Card 2 
Forest floor 
 
Canopy opening change the forest floor. Light increases and soils warm. These changes can 
favor tree seedlings. However, these changes can also favor competing plants that can suppress 
tree regeneration. Prior stocking of advance growth increases success. Damage to saplings dur-
ing operations reduces stocking. Trail layout and machine choice limit harm. Site preparation 
can reset competition locally. Surface treatments expose mineral soil patches. Post -harvest 
browsing can further reduce recruits. Protection  and timing reduce losses. The management 
goal is a net gain in desired juveniles. The design aligns gap size, damage control, and follow-
up tending.

Knowledge Card 3 
Animals 
Harvesting shifts biotic dynamics. Bark beetles increase in recently cut stands. Stressed spruce 
near operations face higher attack risk. Tree mortality rises for several years post -entry. Open 
or sparse habitats attract deer. Deer browsing reduces regener ation and understory diversity. 
Repeated use can degrade structure. Some species decline in selection forests whilew other 
species increase in edges or openings. Monitoring detects species-specific responses. Manage-
ment reduces risk by moderating open area creation, retaining resistant trees, and timing entries. 
The objective is to limit pest amplification and herbivory while keeping structural goals.

Chapter 13: Forest Recovery Process and Restoration After Large-Scale 
Disturbances 
Structured Summary 
This chapter presents the various threats a forest faces. With climate change becoming a more 
and more influential factor in ecosystems around the globe, forest ecosystems are increasingly 
under pressure. Firstly, wind throw is capable of affecting stands in a very negative way: strong 
storms with high wind velocities have the ability to break or uproot large numbers of trees. 
Follwing a wind throw event, pests such as the bark beetle find ideal circumstances for rapid 
and extensive proliferation, thus furt her damaging the forest. However, disturbances can also 
initite succession, as in the case of forest fires which facilitate secondary birch stands. Snow 
damage occurs mostly with wet snow and is more likely to occur in dense and thus thin -
stemmed stands. O verall, the chapter emphasizes the importance of forest management 
measures in order to prevent and mitigate damage from the aforementioned risks.  
Knowledge Card 1 
Wind damage 
Storms create large windthrow areas in which conifers suffer more damage than broadleaves. 
Salvage logging removes downed timber and opens access. Restoration pathways depend on 
treatment. Scarification favors birch dominance at high stem density. Planting  with conifers 
restores cover but can face broadleaf competition. Leaving disturbance legacies to remain in 
the forest supports recovery. Legacies include advanced saplings, downed logs, and stumps. 
Dead wood provides habitat and nursery substrates. Dead w ood also buffers carbon stocks in 
cool climates. Seeding can supplement limited seed dispersal. Monitoring tracks cohort com-
position, stand density, and growth. Management chooses treatments to balance recovery speed, 
diversity, and future structure.

Knowledge Card 2 
Fire 
Historic fires create secondary forests in which birch often dominates early succession. Oak can 
establish in some burned areas. These post-fire plantations can mature into old stands. Norway 
spruce and other conifers persist where planted. Secondary stands may require density control. 
Birch at high density forms slender stems and low growth. Early thinning improves diameter 
and vigor. Strip-clear patterns create grid openings and reduce competition. Removal of dense 
undergrowth enhances natural regeneratio n. Seeding and supplemental planting fill species 
gaps. It is beneficial for diversity to aim for mixed conifer –broadleaf composition. The objec-
tive is stable structure with improved growth and biodiversity.

Knowledge Card 3 
Snow damage 
Wet snow bends and snaps stems – especially young stands are vulnerable to snow damage. 
High density leads to thinner stems which are more likely to fail more under packing snow. 
Lower density reduces breakage risk. Damage patterns reflect canopy stage and  timing. Pre-
leaf-shed events increase accumulation and harm. Management reduces risk through density 
control. Mixed -species composition improves structural resilience. Uneven -aged, multistory 
stands buffer disturbances. Polyculture planting spreads risk a nd therefore reuduces it. Moni-
toring tracks stem losses and recovery. The objective is resistance and rapid rebound under 
changing climate.

Chapter 1: Introduction: Terrestrial Ecology 
Structured Summary 
Chapter 1 starts by defining  physiological ecology as the con nection of environment, plant 
function, and growth in forest production systems. The chapter aims to explain the primary 
physiological processes that drive tree growth and to show how weather and plant condition 
modulate these processes, emphasizing understandin over prescriptive management. The focus 
is intentionally kept on mechanistic, process-first explanations that remain broadly applicable 
because terrestrial plants share core physiological mechanisms . The chapter also outlines the 
analytical toolkit: It presents models to formalize the connection of processes with thewir en-
vironment, mathematics to provide quantitative structure, and statistics to address fit, variabil-
ity, complexity, and uncertainty to establish a foundation for interpreting stand responses and 
predicting growth across varied conditions, giving readers a physiology-centered perspective 
for modeling and quantitative analyses. 
 
Knowledge Card 1 
Forest Goods and Services 
Forests provide various services: from aesthetic value to wood production, they fulfill multiple 
functions. With the rising relevance of renewable fuels, bioenergy plantations have become 
more prevalent. They employ fast-growing species, shorter growth cycles and high population 
densities in order to maximise biomass output. Even though these ecosystems differ substan-
tially from what is commonly defined as a forest, their growth as is determined by the same 
biophysical processes. Additionally, the role of forests in the sequestration of CO2 is becoming 
more imporant as anthropogenic climate change ramps up. The wood produced can then be 
manufactured into products such as the basic materials for building or papermaking.

Knowledge Card 2 
Physiological ecology 
In forest production systems, physiological ecology examines how physiological processes in-
teract with environmental conditions to determine ecosystem state and behavior. The aim is to 
describe key processes involved in tree growth and how weather and plant condition affect 
those processes and thus growth outcomes. Physiological ecology focuses on process under-
standing rather than practical forest management prescriptions . However, the knowledge and 
understanding of such processes can be of great use in the management of forest productio n 
systems. The principles are broadly applicable because the physiological processes governing 
terrestrial plants are largely shared across species.

Knowledge Card 3 
Models  
Models are sets of equations that formalize how physiological processes drive tree and stand 
growth. Three complementary types underpin this framework: empirical models that fit data -
driven relationships within similar systems, process -based models that en code mechanisms 
such as resource capture, carbon balance, and allocation, and hybrids that combine mechanistic 
structure with statistical components. Mathematics is used as an efficient language to represent 
processes, with equations treated as approximations rather than exact truths. Uncertainty arises 
from measurement error, biological variation, parameter and structural choices, and the limits 
of transferring models beyond their calibration context. Good practice demands explicit as-
sumptions, careful parameterization and calibration, independent testing against observations, 

--- PAGE 112 ---
and parsimony to keep models no more complex than necessary. Practitioner judgment remains 
central in judging adequacy and determining when added detail is warranted for decisions. The 
orientation is decisional: link environmental drivers and management ac tions to quantitative 
predictions of growth and carbon storage.

Chapter 2: Weather and Energy Balance 
Structured Summary 
Weather provides the common thread that ties leaf function, canopy energy use, and simple flux 
descriptions into a coherent modeling view. As light, temperature, humidity, and wind rise and 
fall across the day, stomata adjust to balance carbon entry with w ater loss, so photosynthesis 
follows the light curve but is moderated when the air becomes dry or movement of air is weak. 
The energy absorbed by the canopy does not simply accumulate; it must be shed either as heat-
ing the air or as evaporation through transpiration. Wind thins the still air next to leaves, making 
it easier for both heat and moisture to leave, so breezy, well-watered conditions shift the energy 
split toward evaporation, cooling leaves and maintaining favorable temperatures for carbon fix-
ation. Calm or very dry conditions shift that split toward heating, raising canopy temperature 
and changing leaf performance. 
These behaviors can be described using conductance and resistance, a shared language for how 
easily heat, gases, and water vapour move. Stomata and the boundary layer act like adjustable 
pathways: open stomata and thin boundary layers mean high conductance and fast flux; closed 
stomata or thick boundary layers mean high resistance and slow flux. This simple framing lets 
us combine weather inputs with leaf traits to anticipate daily patterns of assimilation and tran-
spiration without losing clarity. When we join these ideas—diurnal driver co-variation, energy 
partitioning, and resistance–conductance pathways—we get practical models that predict car-
bon gain and water use at leaf and canopy scale. They remain simple enough to apply with 
common weather data, yet capture the main controls that shape productivity and water demand 
across changing conditions. 
Knowledge Card 1 
Weather 
Daily weather sets the pace for leaf activity. Light climbs in the morning, peaks near midday, 
and declines toward evening; photosynthesis tends to follow this curve. Air temperature and 
humidity change how dry the air feels, which shifts vapour pressure d eficit and affects how 
much water leaves the leaf. Wind helps move heat and moisture away from surfaces. Stomata 
sit at the center of these links: they open with light to let in carbon dioxide, but they partly close 
when the air gets too dry. Because these  drivers rise and fall together, the real photosynthesis 
pattern is light shaped by temperature, dryness, and wind. Reading this coupling helps predict 
when carbon gain is high and when water loss starts to limit it.

• Stomatal conductance → controls → Photosynthesis

Landsberg, J. J., and Peter J. Sands. Physiological Ecology of Forest Production: Principles, 
Processes and Models. 1st ed., Volume 4, Academic Press/Elsevier, 2011, p. 13-48. 
 
Knowledge Card 2 
Energvy balance 
A canopy absorbs sunlight and must shed that energy as heat or as evaporation. When soil 
moisture is available and air can carry vapour away, more energy goes into evaporation through 
transpiration, which cools leaves and keeps temperatures suitable for photosynthesis. Wind re-
duces the still air around leaves, speeding the removal of heat and moisture, so breezy condi-
tions often shift energy toward evaporation. In calm or very dry air, more energy becomes sen-
sible heat, raising canopy temperature and increasing stress. This shifting split between heating 
and evaporation changes through the day with weather and moisture. It sets how warm the 
canopy becomes and how much water it uses, and it strongly influences the rate at which plants 
can fix carbon.

Knowledge Card 3 
Stomata regulation 
Wihin plants, conductance describes how easily heat, gases, or water vapour move while 
esistance describes how much movement is slowed. Stomata adjust these pathways, opening 
and closing to regulate both carbon dioxide entry for photosynthesis and water vapour exit dur-
ing transpiration. A thin boundary layer and open stomata give high conductance and fast move-
ment. a thick boundary layer or closed stomata create give resistance and slow movement. By 
laying out environmental limits, such as wind effects on t he boundary layer, and biological 
controls, such as stomatal behavior, as simple pathways, we can combine weather inputs and 
leaf traits to predict fluxes.

Chapter 3: Physiological Processes 
Structured Summary 
Leaf carbon gain, water loss, and growth are different faces of the same linked system, and the 
links run through supply –demand and control. On the demand side, chloroplast biochemistry 
uses light and enzymes to fix CO2; on the supply side, CO2 must diffus e through stomata and 
the thin layer of still air at the leaf surface. The actual assimilation rate is the point where these 
two meet, so changes in light, internal CO2, or temperature shift the demand curve, while 
changes in stomatal opening or boundary -layer conditions shift the supply curve. Stomata are 
the main control valve: they open with light to admit CO2, but they close when the air is dry or 
the leaf water status declines, coupling photosynthesis to transpiration and forcing trade -offs 
between carbon gain and water use. A coupled model that solves both sides consistently makes 
these trade-offs explicit and ensures predictions of assimilation and conductance move together. 

--- PAGE 117 ---
Respiration then subtracts from gross carbon gain to pay for maintenance and growth across 
tissues, with rates that rise with temperature and vary by organ and season. The carbohydrates 
left after respiration are allocated among leaves, stems, and roots fo llowing source–sink dy-
namics and allometric constraints. These allocation choices feed back on the system: more 
leaves raise light capture and demand for water, more sapwood supports transport and height 
growth, more fine roots enhance water and nutrient uptake, stabilizing stomatal function. When 
these pieces are connected, the result is a coherent pathway from environment to net growth. It 
clarifies how changes in light, dryness, or plant water status ripple through leaf function to 
whole-plant performance, and it provides a practical structure for scaling from leaves to cano-
pies while keeping the core controls transparent. 
Knowledge Card 1 
Photosynthesis and CO2 
Leaf photosynthesis is powered by light and limited by how fast carbon dioxide can reach the 
chloroplasts. The leaf’s biochemistry fixes CO2 through enzyme -driven reactions, with limits 
that shift between the speed of carboxylation and the regeneration of key substrates. Gas -ex-
change data are often interpreted using supply and demand curves: the supply side reflects dif-
fusion through stomata and the still air next to the leaf, while the demand side reflects the bio-
chemical capacity of the leaf. At low internal CO2, assimilation rises steeply with carboxylation 
efficiency; at higher CO2, photosynthesis levels off as regeneration limits take over. Because 
light, CO2, and temperature all shape these responses, practical models capture their non-linear 
effects and scale leaf behavior to whole canopies with representative foliage cohorts.

• Irradiance

• Carboxylation efficiency

--- PAGE 118 ---

• Substrate regeneration limits ➜ cause ➜ Photosynthetic saturation 
• Supply–demand curves ➜ integrate ➜ Diffusion and biochemistry

Knowledge Card 2 
Stomata conductance 
Stomata conductance connects photosynthesis and transpiration by controlling how easily CO2 
enters and water vapour leaves the leaf. It typically rises with light, falls as vapour pressure 
deficit grows, adjusts to ambient CO2, and is influenced by leaf wa ter status. A coupled mod-
eling approach finds the intersection of two curves: the leaf’s biochemical demand for internal 
CO2 and the physical supply through stomata and the boundary layer. This intersection yields 
consistent predictions of assimilation and conductance under given weather and leaf conditions. 
Because stomata integrate light, humidity, CO2, and water potential, they balance carbon gain 
against water loss. Their behavior must be represented explicitly to move from single leaves to 
canopies, ensuring that whole-stand water use and carbon uptake respond realistically to chang-
ing conditions.

• CO2 concentration ➜ modulates ➜ Stomatal conductance

Landsberg, J. J., and Peter J. Sands. Physiological Ecology of Forest Production: Principles,

Knowledge Card 3 
Respiration and biomass 
Respiration burns carbohydrates to release energy that keeps tissues alive and powers growth 
and transport. Leaf dark respiration is commonly measured alongside photosynthesis and varies 
with tissue type, temperature, and season, reflecting shifts in physi ological activity. At stand 
scale, respiration subtracts from gross carbon gain, so net production depends on both photo-
synthesis and respiration. Biomass allocation spreads available carbohydrates among leaves, 
stems, roots, and storage, shaped by source –sink dynamics and allometric constraints. These 
patterns set leaf area, sapwood and fine root investment, and thus feed back on water uptake 
and photosynthetic capacity. Effective models treat respiration with simple temperature-respon-
sive functions where needed and encode allocation rules that match observed ratios and con-
straints, delivering coherent predictions of growth and carbon storage through time and across 
organs.

• Respiration 
• Carbohydrates 
• Temperature

• Source–sink dynamics

• Leaves ➜ exhibit ➜ Dark respiration alongside photosynthesis 
• Photosynthesis ➜ supplies ➜ Carbohydrates to sinks

Chapter 4: Stand Structure and Dynamics 
Structured Summary 
Stand structure links stem numbers, size distributions, foliage area, and root systems into a 
single, evolving framework that governs growth, competition, and resource capture. Stem pop-
ulations change through mortality and self-thinning, pushing stands toward characteristic den-
sity-size relationships and shifting diameter and height distributions over time. These shifts feed 
into allometry: predictable ratios among biomass pools allow stem size to indicate foliage, 
wood, and root masses, and thus leaf area index and canopy function. Foliage structure and leaf 
area index set how much light can be intercepted and turned into carbon gain, while roots set 
the capacity to capture water and nutrients, directly influencing canopy conductance and stabil-
ity. Arrangement matters: horizontal patterns and density indices connect tree spacing to com-
petition for light and space, altering growth trajectories and stability. Together, these compo-
nents form a feedback network: changes in stem numbers alter light environments; altered fo-
liage and eaf area index change radiation interception and carbon gain; shifts in roots and sap-
wood affect water transport and survival; and allometric constraints keep the whole system 
within biologically consistent bounds. This integrated view provides practical handles for mod-
eling and management, since monitoring size distributions, density, and leaf area index allows 
inference about carbon capture, competition intensity, and future stand development, while in-
terventions like thinning can be framed as controlled changes to stem populations and structural 
feedbacks that modify growth responses.  

--- PAGE 121 ---
Knowledge Card 1 
Stem populations and mortality 
Stand development is driven by changing stem numbers and sizes. As trees grow, competition 
intensifies and small or suppressed stems die, producing characteristic mortality patterns and 
long-term declines in stem density known as self-thinning. These dynamics shift diameter and 
height distributions, often widening size spread and moving stands toward predictable density-
size relationships. The result is a feedback: growing crowns and stems increase resource de-
mand and shading, which accelerates mortality among weaker trees. Self-thinning also sets up-
per bounds to how many stems can coexist at a given mean size, helping foresters anticipate 
when stands will stabilize and how interventions like thinning will alter competition and 
growth. Tracking stem populations and distributions therefore provides a quantitative window 
into future canopy development and the balance between individual growth and stand -level 
yield.

• Stem number 
• Mortality 
• Self-thinning 
• Diameter distribution 
• Height distribution 
• Competition

• Diameter distribution ➜ reflects ➜ Stand development stage 
• Height distribution ➜ responds to ➜ Competition intensity

Processes and Models. 1st ed., Volume 4, Academic Press/Elsevier, 2011, p. 83-114 

--- PAGE 122 ---
Knowledge Card 2 
Allometry and Biomass 
Allometry provides stable links among tree parts, allowing stem size to predict how biomass is 
partitioned into foliage, wood, and roots. These relationships constrain allocation and keep 
growth within biologically consistent ratios, making it possible to estimate leaf area index and 
biomass pools from simple measurements of diameter and height. Because leaf area index sets 
the canopy’s capacity to intercept light, allometry becomes a bridge from structure to function: 
greater leaf area boosts radiation abs orption and potential carbon gain, while increased stem 
and branch mass modifies crown architecture and light distribution. Using established allome-
tric equations, forestry professionals can translate inventory data into functional canopy traits, 
assess productivity potential, and anticipate how changes in tree size or density will ripple 
through foliage and root pools. Allometry thus anchors process-based predictions in measurable 
stand structure.

• Stem size

• LAI ➜ controls ➜ Light interception 
• Biomass pools ➜ influence ➜ Canopy function

Processes and Models. 1st ed., Volume 4, Academic Press/Elsevier, 2011, p. 83-114 
 
Knowledge Card 3 
Foliage structure and roots 
Foliage structure determines how canopies intercept radiation and distribute light among sunlit 
and shaded leaves, shaping photosynthesis and water use. Leaf area index and crown arrange-
ment influence how much energy is absorbed and how efficiently it is used, linking structural 
metrics to canopy productivity. Root systems complete the picture: their mass and depth distri-
bution control water and nutrient uptake, which in turn affect stomatal conductance, transpira-
tion, and resilience to stress. Modeling radiation interception benefits from simple but robust 
treatments that separate direct and diffuse light and recognize heterogeneity within crowns, 
while root information anchors predictions about water supply and nutrient status. Together, 
foliage and roots create a functional envelope around stems, integrating energy capture above-
ground with resource capture belowground and setting the stage for stable, productive stands 
 
Entity List 
• Foliage structure 
• Leaf area index (LAI) 
• Radiation interception 
• Sunlit–shaded leaves 
• Root mass 
• Water and nutrient uptake 
 
Relationship Triplets

• LAI ➜ increases ➜ Absorbed energy 
• Sunlit–shaded leaves ➜ partition ➜ Photosynthetic conditions 
• Root mass ➜ enables ➜ Water and nutrient uptake 
• Water uptake ➜ supports ➜ Stomatal conductance 
• Radiation interception ➜ drives ➜ Canopy productivity

Chapter 8: Modelling Tree Growth 
Structured Summary 
Modeling links leaf biophysics to stand development by turning process understanding into 
testable, quantitative predictions. The connection runs from how we represent mechanisms (re-
source capture, carbon balance, and allocation) to how we fit and test mod els, and finally to 
how we assemble modular sub -models that scale from leaves to stands. Mechanism -oriented 
structures capture supply-demand limits for CO2 and water, while empirical patterns (such as 
size-density relationships and allometry) anchor predic tions in measurable stand traits. These 
elements meet during parameterization and calibration, where clear assumptions, independent 
testing, and sensitivity analysis expose what matters most and what can remain simple, keeping 
models fit for purpose. Modular designs let us reuse mature components for light interception, 
photosynthesis, and water balance, and then pair them with stand structure and allometry to 
translate fluxes into growth. The result is a practical pathway: use common weather and site 
inputs to predict carbon gain and water use, allocate biomass among organs under observed 
constraints, and evolve stem numbers and sizes through competition rules. By choosing the 
right model type (empirical, process -based, or hybrid) for the decision at hand, and matching 
complexity to data and objectives, we can deliver robust forecasts of productivity and dynamics 
without losing transparency or transferability. 
Knowledge Card 1 
Model types 
Forest growth models come in three complementary forms. Empirical models fit observed data 
to predict stand metrics within conditions similar to those used for their development. Process-
based models encode mechanisms such as resource capture, photosynthes is, respiration, allo-
cation, and demography to predict responses to weather, soils, and management. Hybrid models 
combine the two, using process modules to drive changes that are translated into familiar for-
estry outputs through empirical links. Choice depends on purpose and data: empirical tools are 
efficient within known contexts; process-based structures generalize across climates and treat-
ments; hybrids bridge mechanism and management metrics. All three aim to turn weather and 

--- PAGE 125 ---
site inputs into stand predictions, but differ in how they handle transferability, interpretability 
and data demands. 
 
Entity List 
• Empirical model 
• Process-based model 
• Hybrid model 
• Mechanism 
• Prediction 
• Management objective 
 
Relationship Triplets 
• Empirical model ➜ predicts ➜ Stand metrics within calibration domain 
• Process-based model ➜ represents ➜ Physiological mechanisms 
• Hybrid model ➜ links ➜ Process outputs to empirical targets 
• Mechanism ➜ improves ➜ Transferability to new conditions 
• Prediction ➜ supports ➜ Management decisions 
• Management objective ➜ determines ➜ Model choice 
 
Source: 
Landsberg, J. J., and Peter J. Sands. Physiological Ecology of Forest Production: Principles, 
Processes and Models. 1st ed., Volume 4, Academic Press/Elsevier, 2011, p. 223-232 
Knowledge Card 2 
Parameters, calibration and testing 
 
Modeling depends on assumptions, parameterization, and testing. Parameters should be tied to 
measurable traits when possible, then calibrated against independent observations to anchor 
predictions. Validation checks whether the model reproduces patterns be yond its tuning set, 
while sensitivity analysis reveals which parameters and drivers control outputs. These practices 
expose structural limits and guide targeted refinement where added detail pays off. Parsimony 
keeps models as simple as they can be while still answering the intended questions, avoiding 

--- PAGE 126 ---
unused complexity and fragile overfitting. By combining explicit assumptions, transparent pa-
rameter sources, independent tests, and sensitivity-guided refinement, models become reilable. 
 
Entity List 
• Parameters 
• Calibration 
• Validation 
• Sensitivity analysis 
• Assumptions 
• Parsimony 
 
Relationship Triplets 
• Assumptions ➜ frame ➜ Model structure 
• Parameters ➜ are fitted by ➜ Calibration 
• Calibration ➜ is checked by ➜ Validation 
• Sensitivity analysis ➜ identifies ➜ Influential controls 
• Parsimony ➜ limits ➜ Unnecessary complexity 
• Validation ➜ builds ➜ Confidence in predictions 
 
Source: 
Landsberg, J. J., and Peter J. Sands. Physiological Ecology of Forest Production: Principles, 
Processes and Models. 1st ed., Volume 4, Academic Press/Elsevier, 2011, p. 238-240 
 
Knowledge Card 3  
Process-based models 
Process-based models allow for extrapolation from observed situations to others. They can de-
scribe forest growth by simulating the underlying mechanisms that convert environment into 
carbon gain, water use, and biomass change. They take common weather and site inputs—ra-
diation, temperature, vapour pressure deficit, soil water —and drive sub-models for light cap-
ture, photosynthesis, respiration, water balance, and allocation. These mechanisms update state 
variables such as leaf area, biomass pools, and soil m oisture through time, so predictions 
emerge from the interplay of processes rather than fitted trends. Because the structure is causal 

--- PAGE 127 ---
and unit-consistent, the same model can explore new conditions, management actions, or cli-
mates with transparent assumptions. 
 
Entity List 
• Process-based model 
• Mechanisms 
• Environmental drivers 
• Sub-models 
• State variables 
• Predictions 
 
Relationship Triplets

• Environmental drivers ➜ drive ➜ Mechanisms 
• Sub-models ➜ compose ➜ Process-based model 
• Mechanisms ➜ update ➜ State variables 
• State variables ➜ produce ➜ Predictions 
• Process-based model ➜ supports ➜ Scenario analysis

Landsberg, J. J., and Peter J. Sands. Physiological Ecology of Forest Production: Principles, 
Processes and Models. 1st ed., Volume 4, Academic Press/Elsevier, 2011, p. 237-239

Chapter 3: Methods 
Structured Summary 
Chapter 3 lays out the methodology and tools employed in the study at hand. It first presents 
the procedures used to evaluate and asses the risk climatic change poses for forest ecosystems. 
It does so by firstly providing a definition of assesment endpoint s which in turn provide the 
metrics by which risk can be measured and evaluates the measurements thus made. These end-
points need to be relevant from a societal as well as a biological standpoint. Furthermore, they 
ought to be unequivocal in their significa nce for the entity at risk. These indicators are subse-
quently weighted and brought into hierarchical relation in order to constitute a model. 
In order to predict constantly changing environmental conditions, models are such as PICUS 
are employed. The version used in the presented paper is PICUS v1.2. The chapter goes on to 
present the models structure, input parameters and the way these parameters are detremined. In 
addition, the model builds on climate data gathered by a network of weather statikons, the 
measurements of which are also fed into the calculations in order to provide a more extensive 
data basis. 
 
Knowledge Card 1 
Risk assessment procedures 
Risks are events that are related to damage. The fulfillment of forest functions is threathened 
by these risks. Forest ecosystems are heavily influenced by climatic conditions.  
Risk assessment procedure is the first step in a systematic evaluation of climate change impacts 
on forests. It defines clear assessment endpoints. These endpoints represent ecologically rele-
vant damages. Typical endpoints include tree mortality, productiv ity loss, and changes in spe-
cies composition. Indicators quantify the magnitude of these effects. Each indicator is evaluated 

--- PAGE 129 ---
by relevance and sensitivity. Indicators are weighted and aggregated in a hierarchical structure. 
The method separates short- and midterm impacts from long-term impacts. Short- and midterm 
impacts describe transient forest responses. Long -term impacts desc ribe changes in potential 
natural vegetation. Both perspectives are necessary for interpretation. The procedure integrates 
multiple indicators into composite indices. The final result is an overall climate change impact 
index.

Knowledge Card 2 
Forest dynamics model PICUS v1.2 
PICUS v1.2 is a forest dynamics simulation model. It operates at the scale of small forest 
patches. The model simulates individual trees. It represents growth, regeneration, and mortality. 
Tree processes depend on environmental conditions. Key drivers incl ude temperature, water 
availability, light, and nutrients. Competition occurs among trees within a patch. The model 
integrates physiological responses and stand structure. PICUS uses forest inventory, soil, and 
site data for initialization. It simulates forest development under changing climate. It also sim-
ulates long-term balanced states. These states correspond to potential natural vegetation. The 
model is designed for climate impact analysis. It allows comparison between current forests 
and simulated future forests under different scenarios.

Knowledge Card 3 
Climate Data 
Climate data provides the input for forest simulations. The baseline climate represents current 
conditions. It is based on long-term observations. Monthly temperature and precipitation meas-
urements are also used. Data is interpolated from station networks. Climate change scenarios 
describe future conditions. Models provide large-scale climate output which has to be transfer-
rede to local scales via statistical downscaling. The result are site-specific climate time series. 
Transient climate data describes gradual change until mid-century. Stabilized climate data de-
scribes future balanced conditions. Together, this data shows short-term and long-term impacts 
of changing climate to forest ecosystenms . Precipitation and temperature  directly influence 
growth, mortality, and regeneration processes in the forest model.

Chapter 4: Data Results 
Structured Summary 
Chapter 4 presents the climate change scenarios devised using the climate change models such 
as the ones introduced in the previous chapter. The chapter provides graphical depictions of 
austria which illustrate statistical interpolations of precipitation  in summer- and wintertime. 
They also show statistically interpolated graphics of temperatures in austria during winter and 
summer. Root mean square error of these interpolations lies between 0,5° and 2,0° C and be-
tween 10 mm and 40 mm for precipitation. Three scenarios are presented: the main regionalized 
climate change scenario scA and two additional climate scenarios whose purpose it is to 
broaden the analysis of the impact of climate change on forests and ecosystems. 
 
Knowledge Card 1 
Scenario ScA 
Scenario ScA shows a moderate climate change scenario. It is derived from regionalized output 
of the global circulation model ECHAM4. The scenario describes a gradual warming trend until 
the mid-21st century. Mean annual temperature increases by approximat ely 0.8 °C by 2050. 
Warming shows variation over time and location: Summer and winter temperatures increase 
differently across regions. Precipitation changes are spatially heterogeneous. Both increases 

--- PAGE 133 ---
and decreases occur. Annual precipitation changes remain within approximately 7 %. Scenario 
ScA defines the baseline climate change signal for comparison.

Knowledge Card 2 
Scenario ScB 

--- PAGE 134 ---
Scenario ScB represents a strong warming scenario. It is constructed by modifying Scenario 
ScA. The scenario assumes a uniform temperature increase of +2 °C by 2050. Temporal varia-
bility follows the pattern of Scenario ScA. Precipitation remains unchanged relative to ScA. 
The scenario isolates the effect of stronger warming. It excludes additional changes in water 
supply. ScB is used to assess forest sensitivity to temperature stress alone. The scenario in-
creases heat sums across all regions. It intensifies drought risk indirectly through higher evap-
orative demand. ScB produces stronger impacts on forest processes than ScA. It marks a tran-
sition from moderate to severe climate stress.

Knowledge Card 3 
Scenario ScC 
Scenario ScC represents a combined warming and drying scenario. It extends Scenario ScB. 
The scenario assumes a temperature increase of +2 °C by 2050. It includes an additional reduc-
tion in summer precipitation. Summer precipitation decreases by 15% relative to Scenario ScA. 
The reduction applies to the growing season from April to September. ScC represents a worst-
case climate trajectory. It combines thermal stress and water limitation. Soil moisture declines 
strongly under this scenario. Drought frequency  increases. Forest water balance becomes the 
dominant limiting factor. ScC is used to assess compound climate impacts. It highlights vulner-
ability to simultaneous heat and moisture stress. The scenario produces the strongest climatic 
signal among all scenarios.

Chapter 5: Risk assesment results 
Knowledge Card 1 
PNV under current climate 
Simulated potential natural vegetation under current climate represents the equilibrium forest 
composition expected to naturally occur without management. The simulation uses baseline 
climate conditions from 1961–1995. PICUS v1.2 generates long-term stable species composi-
tions. These compositions reflect site conditions, soil properties, and climate. The results de-
scribe the ecological potential of forest sites. Simulated PNV is compared with expert - PNV 
classifications from the Austrian Forest Inventory. If those two match, it indicates model plau-
sibility. PNV under current climate serves as a reference for climate change impact analysis. 
All future scenarios are evaluated relative to this baseline. The simulated PNV reflects altitudi-
nal gradients across Austria.

Knowledge Card 2 
PNV under climate change scenarios 
Simulated PNV under climate change scenarios describes the possible future equilibrium of 
forests. Simulations apply altered temperature and precipitation scenarios. Scenarios ScA, ScB, 
and ScC define increasing climate stress. PICUS v1.2 runs long-term simulations until equilib-
rium is reached. Species composition shifts relative to the baseline climate. Warming favors 
warmth-loving and broadleaved species such as fagus sylvatica. However, cold-adapted species 
decline at lower elevations. Changes intensify with stronger warming and drying. Altitudinal 
belts shift upward. Regional differences remain strong. Simulated PNV reveals changes in eco-
logical site potential. These changes indicate long-term climate impacts independent of current 
stand structure.

Knowledge Card 3 
Forest response to climate change 
The short- to midterm response models measure forest dynamics during climate change. The 
analysis covers the period from 2000 to 2050. Climate scenarios impose gradual warming and 
altered moisture conditions. Forest stands respond before reaching equilibrium. Responses in-
clude changes in growth, vitality, and mortality. Norway spruce outside its ecological optimum 
shows high sensitivity. Picea abies is especially threatened by tree mortality, either by drought 
or bark beetle infestation. Mortality events occur episodically. Responses differ across regions 
and elevations. High elevations show the largest effects in climatic change within species com-
position.  These responses represent immediate climate-related risks to existing forests ecosys-
tems in the future.

Grey literature: Forstliche Etragslehre (Prielinger) 
Block 1: Grundlagen  
1. Knowledge Card 
Title 
Grundlagen der forstlichen Ertragslehre 
Text 
Die forstliche Ertragslehre befasst sich mit der Beschreibung, Analyse und Prognose des 
Wachstums von Waldbäumen und Waldbeständen. Sie untersucht, wie Holzvorrat, Zuwachs 
und Produktion in Abhängigkeit von Zeit, Standort und Bestandesstruktur entstehen. Ziel ist es, 
Wachstumsprozesse quantitativ zu erfassen und für Planung, Bewertung und Steuerung der 
Waldbewirtschaftung nutzbar zu machen. 
Zentrale Größen der Ertragslehre sind der Holzvorrat, der Zuwachs sowie die Gesamtwuchs-
leistung eines Bestandes. Diese Größen ermöglichen Aussagen über Produktivität und Entwick-
lung eines Waldes. Die Ertragslehre verbindet biologische Grundlagen des Baumwa chstums 
mit mathematisch-statistischen Methoden. Da Wachstum das Ergebnis komplexer Wechselwir-
kungen zwischen Klima, Boden, Baumart und Konkurrenz ist, arbeitet die Ertragslehre mit ver-
einfachenden Modellen. Diese Modelle dienen nicht nur der Ertragsprogno se, sondern auch 
dem Verständnis ökologischer Zusammenhänge und der Bewertung waldbaulicher Maßnah-
men.

--- PAGE 142 ---
1. Knowledge Card  
Title 
Wachstum als biologischer Prozess 
Text 
Wachstum bezeichnet die irreversible Zunahme von Biomasse eines Baumes oder Bestandes 
über die Zeit. Es beruht auf physiologischen Prozessen wie Photosynthese, Atmung und 
Stofftransport. Die Photosynthese stellt die energetische Grundlage dar, indem sie Lichtenergie 
in chemische Energie umwandelt und organische Substanz aufbaut. Ein Teil dieser Energie 
wird durch Atmung wieder verbraucht, während der verbleibende Anteil dem Aufbau von Holz, 
Blättern und Wurzeln dient. 
Das tatsächliche Wachstum wird durch äußere Faktoren begrenzt. Dazu zählen Licht, Wasser, 
Nährstoffe und Temperatur. Zusätzlich wirken Konkurrenzprozesse innerhalb des Bestandes 
regulierend, da mehrere Bäume um dieselben Ressourcen konkurrieren. Wachstum i st daher 
kein konstanter Prozess, sondern reagiert sensibel auf Umweltbedingungen und Bestan-
desstruktur. Die Ertragslehre abstrahiert diese biologischen Prozesse, um Wachstum messbar, 
vergleichbar und prognostizierbar zu machen.

1. Knowledge Card  
Title 
Messgrößen des Waldwachstums 
Text 
Um Waldwachstum beschreiben und vergleichen zu können, werden in der forstlichen Ertrags-
lehre standardisierte Messgrößen verwendet. Zentrale Wachstumsgrößen sind der Holzvorrat 
und der Zuwachs eines Waldbestandes. Der Holzvorrat beschreibt die zu einem bes timmten 
Zeitpunkt vorhandene Holzmenge und wird meist als Volumen pro Fläche angegeben. Der Zu-
wachs bezeichnet die zeitliche Veränderung dieses Vorrates und kann für einzelne Bäume oder 
ganze Bestände ermittelt werden.  
Unterschieden werden verschiedene Zuw achsarten, darunter der laufende Zuwachs und der 
durchschnittliche Zuwachs. Diese Kennwerte erlauben Aussagen über die aktuelle Wachstums-
leistung sowie über langfristige Entwicklungstendenzen. Da Wachstum räumlich und zeitlich 
variiert, sind Messgrößen ste ts an Bezugsflächen und Zeiträume gebunden. Die Verwendung 
einheitlicher Messgrößen ist eine zentrale Voraussetzung für die Vergleichbarkeit von Bestän-
den, Versuchsflächen und Wachstumsmodellen innerhalb der forstlichen Ertragslehre.

--- PAGE 145 ---
Structured Summary  
Waldwachstum bildet die biologische Grundlage der forstlichen Ertragslehre und beschreibt die 
zeitliche Zunahme von Biomasse bei Einzelbäumen und Waldbeständen. Ziel der Ertragslehre 
ist es, diese Wachstumsprozesse zu erfassen, zu erklären und für forstlic he Entscheidungen 
nutzbar zu machen. Zentrale Kenngrößen sind Holzvorrat, Zuwachs und Gesamtwuchsleistung, 
die gemeinsam Aussagen über Produktivität und Entwicklung eines Bestandes ermöglichen. 
Das Wachstum beruht auf physiologischen Prozessen, insbesondere auf der Photosynthese als 
Quelle der Biomasseproduktion. Der Aufbau organischer Substanz wird durch Atmung teil-
weise wieder reduziert, sodass nur ein Teil der produzierten Energie dem Wachstum  zur Ver-
fügung steht. Das tatsächliche Wachstum wird durch Umweltfaktoren wie Licht, Wasser, Nähr-
stoffe und Temperatur begrenzt. Zusätzlich wirken Konkurrenzprozesse innerhalb des Bestan-
des regulierend, da mehrere Bäume um dieselben Ressourcen konkurrieren. 
Da Waldwachstum das Ergebnis komplexer biologischer und ökologischer Wechselwirkungen 
ist, arbeitet die Ertragslehre mit Modellen. Diese vereinfachen die Realität, um Wachstum ver-
gleichbar und prognostizierbar zu machen. Wachstumsmodelle dienen nicht nur der Ertragsab-
schätzung, sondern auch dem Verständnis ökologischer Zusammenhänge und der Bewertung 
waldbaulicher Maßnahmen. Damit stellt die forstliche Ertragslehre eine zentrale Schnittstelle 
zwischen Biologie, Ökologie und forstlicher Praxis dar. 
 
  

--- PAGE 146 ---
Block 2: Produktivität und Wachstumspotenzial von Wäldern 
1. Knowledge Card  
Title 
Produktivität von Waldökosystemen 
Text 
Die Produktivität eines Waldökosystems beschreibt die Fähigkeit, Biomasse durch biologische 
Prozesse aufzubauen. Sie ist ein Maß für die Leistungsfähigkeit eines Standortes und eines Be-
standes. In der forstlichen Ertragslehre wird Produktivität genutzt, um Wachstumspotenziale zu 
vergleichen und langfristige Ertragsaussagen abzuleiten. Produktivität ist das Ergebnis des Zu-
sammenwirkens von Klima, Boden, Baumart und Bestandesstruktur. 
Ein hoher Produktivitätswert bedeutet nicht zwangsläufig hohen Holzertrag, da Verluste durch 
Atmung, Mortalität oder Nutzung berücksichtigt werden müssen. Dennoch stellt Produktivität 
die energetische Grundlage aller Wachstumsprozesse dar. In der Ertragslehre dient sie als Bin-
deglied zwischen ökologischen Prozessen und messbaren Wachstumsgrößen wie Vorrat und 
Zuwachs. Produktivität ist somit keine direkt beobachtbare Größe, sondern wird über Model-
lansätze und Kenngrößen abgeleitet.

--- PAGE 148 ---
 
1. Knowledge Card  
Title 
Bruttoprimärproduktion (BPP) 
Text 
Die Bruttoprimärproduktion beschreibt die gesamte durch Photosynthese erzeugte organische 
Substanz eines Ökosystems innerhalb eines bestimmten Zeitraumes. Sie stellt die maximale 
potenzielle Biomasseproduktion dar, bevor Verluste durch Atmung berücksichtig t werden. In 
Waldökosystemen wird die BPP maßgeblich durch Lichtangebot, Blattfläche und photosynthe-
tische Effizienz bestimmt. 
Die BPP ist eine theoretische Größe, da sie nicht direkt messbar ist. Stattdessen wird sie mithilfe 
von Modellen oder indirekten Messmethoden abgeschätzt. Sie bildet die energetische Aus-
gangsbasis für alle weiteren Stoff - und Energieflüsse im Wald. Verände rungen der BPP spie-
geln Änderungen klimatischer Bedingungen oder struktureller Eigenschaften des Bestandes wi-
der. In der Ertragslehre dient die BPP vor allem dem Verständnis der biologischen Wachstums-
grenzen eines Standortes.

--- PAGE 150 ---
1. Knowledge Card  
Title 
Nettoprimärproduktion (NPP) 
Text  
Die Nettoprimärproduktion bezeichnet den Anteil der Bruttoprimärproduktion, der nach Abzug 
der Atmungsverluste verbleibt. Sie stellt jene Biomasse dar, die tatsächlich für Wachstum, Spei-
cherung und Reproduktion zur Verfügung steht. Die NPP ist damit eine zentrale Größe für die 
Beurteilung der realen Produktivität eines Waldökosystems. 
In Wäldern verteilt sich die NPP auf verschiedene Baumorgane wie Stamm, Äste, Blätter und 
Wurzeln. Nur ein Teil dieser Biomasse geht in langfristig nutzbares Holz über. Die Höhe der 
NPP wird von Standortfaktoren, Baumarten, Bestandesalter und Konkurrenz be einflusst. Da 
die NPP näher an der tatsächlich verfügbaren Biomasse liegt als die BPP, besitzt sie eine hohe 
Bedeutung für forstliche Ertragsabschätzungen und ökologische Analysen. 
 
2. Entity List

--- PAGE 152 ---
1. Knowledge Card  
Title 
Einflussfaktoren auf die Produktivität 
Text  
Die Produktivität von Wäldern wird durch eine Vielzahl von Einflussfaktoren bestimmt. Zu 
den wichtigsten gehören klimatische Bedingungen wie Strahlung, Temperatur und Nieder-
schlag. Sie steuern die Photosyntheseleistung sowie die Länge der Vegetationsperiode. Boden-
faktoren beeinflussen vor allem die Wasser- und Nährstoffversorgung der Bäume. 
Neben abiotischen Faktoren spielen biotische Einflüsse eine zentrale Rolle. Baumart, Bestan-
desdichte und Altersstruktur bestimmen, wie effizient Ressourcen genutzt werden. Konkurrenz-
prozesse können die Produktivität einzelner Bäume verringern, während sie auf Bestandes-
ebene teilweise kompensiert werden. Auch waldbauliche Eingriffe verändern die Produktivität, 
indem sie Bestandesstruktur und Ressourcenzugänglichkeit beeinflussen. Produktivität ist da-
her stets das Ergebnis eines komplexen Zusammenspiels mehrerer Faktoren.

Structured Summary 
Produktivität beschreibt die Fähigkeit eines Waldökosystems, Biomasse aufzubauen, und bildet 
die energetische Grundlage aller Wachstumsprozesse. Sie ergibt sich aus dem Zusammenspiel 
von Klima, Boden, Baumart und Bestandesstruktur und variiert räumlich und  zeitlich. In der 
forstlichen Ertragslehre dient Produktivität dazu, Wachstumspotenziale zu vergleichen und 
ökologische Grenzen der Holzproduktion zu verstehen. 
Die Bruttoprimärproduktion stellt die gesamte durch Photosynthese erzeugte Biomasse dar und 
beschreibt das theoretische Maximum der biologischen Produktion. Sie ist stark vom Lichtan-
gebot, von der Blattfläche und von klimatischen Bedingungen abhängig. Da V erluste durch 
Atmung noch nicht berücksichtigt sind, ist die BPP keine direkt nutzbare Wachstumsgröße. 
Die Nettoprimärproduktion berücksichtigt diese Verluste und beschreibt jene Biomasse, die 
tatsächlich für Wachstum, Speicherung und Reproduktion zur Verfügung steht. Sie stellt damit 
eine zentrale Größe für die Beurteilung der realen Produktivität von Wäld ern dar. Die NPP 
verteilt sich auf verschiedene Baumorgane und wird durch Standort, Baumart, Alter und Kon-
kurrenz beeinflusst. 
Produktivität wird zusätzlich durch waldbauliche Eingriffe verändert, da diese die Bestan-
desstruktur und Ressourcennutzung steuern. Insgesamt zeigt Block 2, dass Produktivität keine 
feste Größe ist, sondern das Ergebnis komplexer ökologischer und waldbaulicher Wechselwir-
kungen. 
  

--- PAGE 155 ---
Block 3: Allometrie, Wachstumsgesetze und Skalierung 
1. Knowledge Card 
Title 
Allometrie im Waldwachstum 
Text  
Allometrie beschreibt gesetzmäßige Beziehungen zwischen verschiedenen Größen eines Bau-
mes, etwa zwischen Durchmesser, Höhe, Kronenvolumen oder Biomasse. Diese Beziehungen 
beruhen auf biologischen Wachstumsprinzipien und ermöglichen es, schwer messbare Größen 
aus leicht erfassbaren Merkmalen abzuleiten. In der forstlichen Ertragslehre spielt die Allomet-
rie eine zentrale Rolle, da sie die Grundlage vieler Wachstums - und Biomassemodelle bildet. 
Allometrische Beziehungen sind meist artspezifisch, können aber au ch durch Standortbedin-
gungen und Bestandesstruktur beeinflusst werden. Häufig werden sie in Form von Potenzfunk-
tionen dargestellt, die nichtlineare Zusammenhänge zwischen Baumdimensionen beschreiben. 
Mithilfe allometrischer Gleichungen lassen sich Volumen, Biomasse oder Blattfläche einzelner 
Bäume schätzen. Damit stellen allometrische Modelle ein wichtiges Bindeglied zwischen bio-
logischer Realität und quantitativer Modellierung dar.

--- PAGE 157 ---
1. Knowledge Card 
Title 
Wachstumsgesetze von Bäumen 
Text 
Wachstumsgesetze beschreiben typische Verlaufsformen des Baum- und Bestandeswachstums 
über die Zeit. Häufig folgt das Wachstum einer S-förmigen Kurve, bei der anfänglich langsames 
Wachstum in eine Phase starken Zuwachses übergeht und später wieder abnimmt.  Diese Ent-
wicklung spiegelt physiologische Grenzen, zunehmende Konkurrenz und altersbedingte Ver-
änderungen wider.  
Mathematische Wachstumsfunktionen wie die Chapman -Richards-Funktion oder logistische 
Modelle werden genutzt, um diese Verläufe zu beschreiben.  Sie ermöglichen es, Wachstum 
zeitlich zu glätten und charakteristische Parameter wie maximale Wuchsleistung oder Wende-
punkte zu bestimmen. Wachstumsgesetze stellen keine exakten Abbildungen der Realität dar, 
sondern idealisierte Annäherungen. Dennoch sind sie ein zentrales Werkzeug der Ertragslehre, 
da sie Vergleiche zwischen Beständen und Standorten erlauben und Prognosen über zukünftige 
Entwicklungen ermöglichen.

--- PAGE 159 ---
1. Knowledge Card 
Title 
Skalierung von Wachstum und Biomasse 
Text 
Skalierung beschreibt, wie Wachstums- und Strukturgrößen vom Einzelbaum auf den Bestand 
oder das Ökosystem übertragen werden. Da Prozesse auf Baumebene nicht linear auf größere 
Ebenen übertragbar sind, erfordert Skalierung spezielle Annahmen und Modelle. In der forstli-
chen Ertragslehre ist sie notwendig, um Einzelbauminformationen in Bestandeskennwerte wie 
Vorrat oder Gesamtbiomasse zu überführen.  
Skalierungsansätze basieren häufig auf allometrischen Beziehungen und statistischen Aggrega-
tionen. Dabei können  Fehler entstehen, wenn die strukturelle Heterogenität eines Bestandes 
nicht ausreichend berücksichtigt wird. Eine korrekte Skalierung ist daher entscheidend für rea-
listische Wachstumsprognosen. Sie verbindet Messungen auf Baumebene mit Aussagen zur 
Produktivität ganzer Bestände oder Landschaften.

--- PAGE 161 ---
Structured Summary 
Allometrie, Wachstumsgesetze und Skalierung bilden zentrale konzeptionelle Grundlagen der 
forstlichen Ertragslehre. Allometrische Beziehungen beschreiben gesetzmäßige Zusammen-
hänge zwischen Baumdimensionen und ermöglichen es, wichtige Wachstumsgrößen wie B io-
masse oder Volumen aus einfach messbaren Merkmalen abzuleiten. Sie stellen damit eine wich-
tige Verbindung zwischen biologischen Wachstumsprozessen und quantitativer Modellierung 
dar. 
Wachstumsgesetze beschreiben typische zeitliche Verläufe des Baum - und Bestandeswachs-
tums. Diese Verläufe spiegeln physiologische Grenzen, zunehmende Konkurrenz sowie alters-
abhängige Veränderungen wider. Mathematische Wachstumsfunktionen dienen dazu, diese  
Muster zu vereinfachen und vergleichbar zu machen. Obwohl sie idealisierte Annäherungen 
darstellen, ermöglichen sie Prognosen und Vergleiche zwischen Beständen und Standorten. 
Die Skalierung verbindet Prozesse auf Einzelbaumebene mit Aussagen über Bestände oder grö-
ßere räumliche Einheiten. Da Wachstumsprozesse nicht linear skalieren, sind geeignete Mo-
delle erforderlich, die strukturelle Heterogenität berücksichtigen. Eine fehler hafte Skalierung 
kann zu erheblichen Abweichungen in Wachstums- und Ertragsprognosen führen. Block 3 ver-
deutlicht damit die Bedeutung mathematischer und konzeptioneller Werkzeuge für das Ver-
ständnis und die Modellierung von Waldwachstum. 
 
  

--- PAGE 162 ---
Block 4: Bestandeswachstum, Wuchsreihen, Ertragstafeln und Bonität 
1. Knowledge Card 
Bestandeswachstum 
Text 
Bestandeswachstum beschreibt die zeitliche Entwicklung eines Waldbestandes als Ganzes und 
ergibt sich aus dem Zusammenwirken des Wachstums einzelner Bäume. Im Gegensatz zum 
Einzelbaumwachstum stehen hierbei flächenbezogene Größen wie Vorrat, Grundfläche un d 
Zuwachs im Vordergrund. Bestandeswachstum wird wesentlich durch Alter, Standortqualität, 
Bestandesdichte und Baumartenzusammensetzung beeinflusst.  
Mit zunehmendem Alter verändert sich die Wachstumsdynamik eines Bestandes: In jungen 
Phasen steigt der Zuwa chs stark an, erreicht ein Maximum und nimmt in späteren Entwick-
lungsstadien wieder ab. Gleichzeitig wirken Konkurrenzprozesse regulierend auf das Wachs-
tum einzelner Bäume, während der Gesamtzuwachs des Bestandes oft stabil bleibt. Bestan-
deswachstum bildet die Grundlage für Ertragsprognosen und stellt einen zentralen Bezugspunkt 
für waldbauliche Entscheidungen dar.

--- PAGE 164 ---
1. Knowledge Card 
Title 
Wuchsreihen und Entwicklungstypen 
Text  
Wuchsreihen beschreiben typische Entwicklungsverläufe von Waldbeständen unter vergleich-
baren Standortbedingungen. Sie ordnen Bestände nach ihrer Produktivität und zeigen, wie sich 
Höhe, Vorrat und Zuwachs mit zunehmendem Alter entwickeln. Wuchsreihen stell en damit 
eine systematische Grundlage für den Vergleich verschiedener Bestände dar.  
Innerhalb einer Wuchsreihe folgen Bestände ähnlichen Wachstumsmustern, unterscheiden sich 
jedoch durch ihre Bonität. Höhere Bonität führt zu schnellerem Höhen - und Volumenwachs-
tum, während geringere Bonität flachere Wachstumsverläufe aufweist. Wuchsreihen abstrahie-
ren reale Wachstumsprozesse und dienen als Bindeglied zwischen empirischen Beobachtungen 
und Ertragsmodellen. 
 
2. Entity List
